SourcesNOTUS (Sept. 1 — Dhillon recruitment email; single-outlet) · Votebeat (July 7) · KJZZ (July 7) · Arizona Mirror (letter PDF) · SOS Fontes statement · NBC News · Democracy Docket (monitors) · Democracy Docket (Maricopa) · KTAR · KJZZ (July 9, monitors) · Newsweek (monitor map) · CBS News (state responses) · Arizona Mirror (July 11, observer training) · AZ Capitol Times (July 15) · Fontes letter (PDF) · KJZZ (July 15) · Democracy Docket (ICE) · AZ Capitol Times (July 17, LUCHA) · AP Decision Notes (July 20) · MyNewsLA (Aug. 28) · PJ Media (Aug. 28) · Fox News (Aug. 28) · AP via WSLS (Aug. 29)

CriticalTrump Admin

DHS Secretary Mullin Threatens Election Officials With Fines, Grant Cutoffs, and “Even… Prison Time” Over SAVE Compliance

DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin · Press remarks and letters to four secretaries of state July 17 · Response demanded within two weeks (~July 31) · New grant-conditioning plan with Commerce announced

Hours after the president’s July 16 address — and one day after DOJ’s sixteenth straight voter-roll court loss — DHS Secretary Markwayne Mullin said on Friday, July 17 that election officials who refuse to run their voter rolls through the SAVE database “can also be held accountable by fines, by penalties, and even… prison time,” with reported figures of fines up to $250,000 and up to five years in prison, plus loss of federal election-related grants for noncompliant states. He claimed SAVE had preliminarily identified 250,000+ noncitizens registered in California, New Jersey, Nevada, and Pennsylvania — offering no methodology; a White House official told CEIR’s David Becker the figure rests on commercial-data comparisons, which Becker called “irresponsible” — and DHS sent letters to those four secretaries of state demanding a response within two weeks (~July 31). Mullin also announced DHS, with the Commerce Department, will make voting-machine “security enhancements” mandatory by conditioning election-related state grants — a new funding lever separate from the FEMA HSGP conditions. Mullin’s remarks repeatedly conflated the SAVE Act (legislation) with the SAVE database (the DHS system two federal courts are fighting over).

What it meansArizona is not among the four named states — but the threat lands here anyway. SOS Fontes is already among the officials threatened by DOJ’s July 7 prosecution letters and has publicly declined DOJ “assistance”; Maricopa’s SAVE-based citizenship investigations are already tracked here; and the D.D.C. injunction currently blocking the expanded SAVE system is the very order DHS is asking the D.C. Circuit to stay, possibly today. A cabinet secretary threatening prison for officials who decline to use a database a federal court has ruled unlawful — the day before a statewide primary run by those officials — is the sharpest official-intimidation escalation this dashboard has tracked, and the grant-conditioning announcement weaponizes exactly the funding streams the standing funding-gap item shows Arizona’s counties depend on.

Update (July 22)No follow-through yet. As of this compile, none of the four named secretaries of state (California, Nevada, New Jersey, Pennsylvania) has publicly responded, DHS has issued no new letters, and the threat has not expanded to Arizona; responses aren't due until ~July 31. The DHS/Commerce grant-conditioning plan remains unformalized, and the D.C. Circuit had not ruled on DHS's motion to stay the SAVE injunction (see the citizenship-investigations item).

What to watchWhether DHS extends the letters beyond the four named states (and to Arizona); the ~July 31 response deadline; how the DHS/Commerce grant-conditioning plan is formalized and whether it is challenged; whether Heather Honey’s DHS election-integrity office (see the White House briefing item) executes the campaign; state AG responses, including AG Mayes.

SourcesNBC News (July 17) · Democracy Docket (July 17) · DHS release (July 17) · ABC News · Washington Times (July 17) · Al Jazeera (July 18)

ElevatedTrump Admin

FEMA Ties $1.06 Billion in Terrorism-Prevention Grants to Election-Policy Compliance

FEMA FY2026 Homeland Security Grant Program · NOFO released July 9 · DHS press release July 10 · Applications due July 24 · 20% holdback (updated)

FEMA’s FY2026 Homeland Security Grant Program notice — the $1.064 billion terrorism-prevention program that funds state and urban-area security — will withhold 20% of each state’s award until FEMA verifies compliance with new “Election Security” conditions: transition plans away from QR-code/barcode vote-count systems to hand-marked paper ballots, 5% manual audits, voter/ballot reconciliation, SAVE citizenship checks of the entire state voter database within 120 days, and citizenship checks on poll workers and vendors. The SAVE requirement collides directly with the D.D.C. injunction against the revamped SAVE system (see the citizenship-investigations item), and the tabulation conditions would implicate Arizona counties’ current equipment. Applications are due July 24.

What it meansThis is funding-as-leverage arriving at the standing funding-gap story: with the county HAVA appropriation lapsed and federal HAVA grants facing a two-thirds cut, DHS is now conditioning unrelated security money on election-policy changes no Arizona statute requires — putting the state and counties between federal grant dollars on one side and state law plus court orders on the other.

Update (July 13)The conditions are now official policy, not just a reported notice. FEMA formally released the FY2026 HSGP funding notice on July 9, and DHS followed with a July 10 press release framing five “common-sense” election-security requirements — the QR/barcode-to-hand-marked-paper transition plans, 5% manual audits, voter/ballot reconciliation, and SAVE-based citizenship verification extending to poll workers and election-system operators — with 20% of each state’s award withheld until compliance is verified. Election lawyers (including David Becker) predict courts will block the conditions, but no lawsuit had been filed as of July 13 — and no public response from Gov. Hobbs, SOS Fontes, or AG Mayes had surfaced, a gap to watch with the application deadline of July 24 eleven days out. The SAVE requirement still collides with the D.D.C. injunction, which was reaffirmed July 8 (see the citizenship-investigations item). Severity raised to Elevated: a hard compliance clock is now running against grant money Arizona’s counterterrorism programs depend on.

Update (July 15)Congressional oversight opened. On July 13, Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi (D-IL) sent the Acting FEMA Administrator a letter calling the conditions “coercive and unconstitutional” and drawn from Project 2025, and demanding records by July 29 — including which states or urban areas have already had funds withheld and any legal opinion FEMA relied on. Still no public response from Gov. Hobbs, SOS Fontes, or AG Mayes, and no lawsuit against the notice, with the July 24 application deadline now nine days out. (Rep. Krishnamoorthi release (July 13) · Democracy Docket)

Update (July 22)The application deadline is here. FEMA's July 24 HSGP application deadline is two days out, and as of this compile no public position from Gov. Hobbs, SOS Fontes, or AG Mayes on the election-security conditions has surfaced, and no lawsuit specifically challenging the July 2026 election conditions has been filed (earlier AZ/multistate suits targeted the separate immigration/"sanctuary" grant conditions — do not conflate). Rep. Krishnamoorthi's oversight records demand comes due July 29. The 20% holdback and the SAVE-check condition — which collides with the still-in-force D.D.C. injunction — remain unresolved as the clock runs.

Update (July 24)The answer arrived through the courts, on deadline eve. On July 23 — one day before today's application deadline — Arizona joined a 25-state-plus-D.C. coalition (led by Rhode Island, with California, New Jersey, and Illinois) suing FEMA and DHS in federal court (D.R.I.) to block the election conditions as violations of the Administrative Procedure Act and the Constitution's Spending Clause. AG Kris Mayes is a named plaintiff; the complaint targets exactly the conditions tracked here — SAVE citizenship checks of the full voter roll and poll workers, a forced transition to hand-marked paper ballots, and federally prescribed audits and ballot reconciliation — arguing Congress never authorized DHS or FEMA to rewrite state election law. The states estimate FEMA could withhold at least 20% — about $148 million across the plaintiff states — of Homeland Security Grant awards, and could terminate awards entirely for continued noncompliance. (No Arizona-specific dollar figure was published for this suit; $148M is the coalition-wide estimate. This is a distinct case from the states' earlier suits over FEMA's immigration/"sanctuary" conditions — do not conflate.) The suit is a counterweight, not a resolution: the 20% holdback and the SAVE-check condition's collision with the still-in-force D.D.C. injunction remain live pending a ruling. (CBS News (July 23) · Reuters/U.S. News (July 23) · Courthouse News (July 23) · NOTUS (July 23))

Update (Aug. 18)A second front opened — this time from local governments. On Aug. 17–18, four localities — Nashville/Davidson County (TN), Harris County (TX), El Paso County (TX), and Columbus (OH) — filed a separate federal suit challenging the same DHS/FEMA scheme, which withholds 20% of Homeland Security Grant Program funds unless jurisdictions adopt the administration’s election changes (equipment bans, prescribed audits/ballot rules, and running rolls through the misfiring SAVE database). Counsel includes the Brennan Center, Protect Democracy, and the Public Rights Project. It runs parallel to the 25-state-plus-D.C. suit Arizona’s AG Mayes joined July 23; neither case has produced a ruling yet, and the 20% holdback and SAVE-check condition remain live. The added litigation improves the odds the conditions are blocked before they bite this fall, but the coercion of election-security funding Arizona’s counties rely on is unresolved. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 18) · WSMV (Aug. 18))

What to watchWhether the court issues a TRO or preliminary injunction before the holdback bites this fall; how AZDOHS handles the conditions in the interim; whether Hobbs, Fontes, or Mayes take a further public position; interaction with the SAVE litigation and the Senate FSGG/HAVA markup; FEMA's response to Rep. Krishnamoorthi's July 29 records demand.

Update (Aug. 31) — a second set of conditions on the same grant programDHS layered new demands onto HSGP in a Mullin letter dated Aug. 20 (sent Aug. 24, reported Aug. 28): full awards now also require random post-election manual counts of paper ballots and plans to abandon barcode/QR vote counting, on top of the July 9 election-policy conditions and the 20% holdback already tracked here. The letter was drafted by an election-denial litigator now employed at DHS — details in the new top item. No ruling had issued as of Aug. 31 in either AG Mayes’s 25-state suit or the four-locality D.D.C. suit filed Aug. 17–18 (Nashville/Davidson, Harris Co., El Paso Co., Columbus), and no amended complaint adding the new conditions has been reported.

SourcesDemocracy Docket (July 7) · FEMA FY2026 HSGP NOFO · Election Law Blog · DHS release (July 10) · AP/U.S. News (July 10) · Washington Examiner

CriticalTrump Admin

White House Fires Every Remaining EAC Commissioner, Leaving the Agency Empty Before the Midterms

U.S. Election Assistance Commission · Hicks and Hovland fired, McCormick resigned July 9 · Zero commissioners, no quorum · July 9–10, 2026

On July 9, the White House fired the EAC’s two Democratic commissioners, Thomas Hicks and Benjamin Hovland, by evening email — and Republican commissioner Christy McCormick resigned the same night — leaving the U.S. Election Assistance Commission with zero members and no quorum months before the midterms. The agency certifies voting equipment, maintains the federal voter-registration form, and administers HAVA Election Security Grants. Reporting ties the purge to the commissioners’ resistance to adding a documentary proof-of-citizenship requirement to the federal form — the change Trump’s March 2025 elections executive order directed and the EAC declined to make — with the removals enabled by the Supreme Court’s June ruling expanding presidential removal power over independent agencies. Replacements require Senate confirmation; none have been named.

What it meansFew states are more exposed than Arizona. Arizona’s bifurcated “federal-only” registration system hinges on the federal form the EAC controls — the same form at the center of RNC v. Mi Familia Vota, to be argued this fall — and roughly 50,000 federal-only voters depend on it. A repopulated or bypassed EAC could attempt to add citizenship-documentation requirements administratively; in the meantime, voting-system certification and HAVA grant administration are in limbo, compounding the funding gaps and CISA pullback already tracked here. Election Law Blog flags the risk that the administration asserts EAC powers over the federal form directly.

Update (July 15)The empty commission now backstops a bigger move: President Trump’s July 16 primetime address will allege voting-machine “vulnerabilities” (see the new primetime-address item), and reporting notes he previously tried to order the EAC — which certifies voting systems — to decertify machines nationwide. With no commissioners and no quorum, the agency can neither resist nor act, and no replacements have been named as of July 15. (Democracy Docket (July 14))

What to watchWhether loyalist replacements are named or the administration tries to exercise EAC functions without a quorum; litigation over the firings; effects on pending HAVA disbursements and voting-system certifications; interaction with the Supreme Court proof-of-citizenship case.

SourcesVotebeat (July 9) · NPR (July 9) · CNN (July 9) · Roll Call (July 10) · Democracy Docket · ProPublica · Election Law Blog

EmergingElection Admin

Apache County’s First Election Without Spanish on Printed Ballots — Gila Follows

Apache and Gila County boards · Supervisor votes earlier in 2026 · First live election July 21 · Reported July 8–13, 2026

The July 21 primary is the first election since Apache County’s supervisors voted unanimously earlier this year to remove Spanish translations from printed ballots — and Gila County has made the same move, per July 8 reporting. Both counties say Spanish remains available on in-person accessible voting machines, and neither is among the four Arizona counties federally required to provide Spanish-language materials (Maricopa, Pima, Yuma, and Santa Cruz). The change takes live effect in majority-Navajo Apache County — which is also one of the three Arizona counties where DOJ election monitors will be present on July 21.

What it meansA quiet reduction in language access gets its first real-world test during a federally monitored primary. Any confusion among Spanish-speaking voters — or friction around the machines that now carry the only Spanish-language ballot access — lands in exactly the counties under the most scrutiny, and could feed both access harms and delegitimization narratives.

What to watchVoter-assistance complaints or access problems in Apache and Gila on July 21; whether other non-covered counties follow; any federal Voting Rights Act Section 208 (voter assistance) issues; how county recorders staff bilingual support at vote centers.

SourcesKJZZ (July 13) · AZ Family (July 8)

No items match the current filters. Try resetting or broadening the criteria.

Holding the line

Bright spots

  • Arizona CEOs took the economic case for election legitimacy into the state press — under their own names. On Sept. 14 the Arizona Capitol Times ran a feature on Arizona business leaders organizing against election-result denial ahead of November, built around John Fees of GradGuard and Adam Goodman of Goodmans, who are recruiting signatories for Leadership Now’s “Election Legitimacy Is an Economic Imperative” statement — 60 signatories as of publication. The argument they made is the one this tracker exists to support: contested or delegitimized results are a business risk, bad for investment, for employees and for communities, not an abstract civic concern. The piece also describes Leadership Now’s political-risk framework by its components — voter confidence, mail-ballot restrictions, threats to poll workers, and federal officers at polling locations — which is the same framework the risk items in this dashboard are organized against, and it cites Center for the Future of Arizona survey findings that 65% of likely Arizona voters are confident in the state’s election security while 54% of likely Republican voters are not. That gap is the work. Note one correction for the record: the article misspells LNP CEO Daniella Ballou-Aares’s first name. (Arizona Capitol Times (Sept. 14) · Leadership Now — statement and signatories (primary))
  • The Supreme Court refused to revive the mail-ballot rule — and said the government is likely to lose. On Sept. 14, in USPS v. California, No. 26A305, the Court denied the government’s emergency stay in a short unsigned order that went past the equities to the merits: “The Government is unlikely to succeed on the merits of its challenge to the District Court’s preliminary injunction. And the equitable factors applicable for obtaining emergency relief from this Court do not favor a stay.” Justice Kavanaugh concurred alone, writing that applying the rule in 2026 “would be arbitrary and capricious in violation of the Administrative Procedure Act because state and local election officials do not have sufficient time to reasonably implement the rule before the elections.” Justice Alito, joined by Justice Thomas, dissented. Three weeks earlier the same Court had let the executive order proceed 6–3 on ripeness grounds; this is the opposite result on a developed record. Arizona’s Oct. 7 mailing of roughly 3.2 million ballots is protected, and the corresponding risk item steps down from Critical to Elevated (Order and opinions, No. 26A305 (primary PDF) · NPR (Sept. 14) · NBC News (Sept. 14)).
  • A unanimous First Circuit panel refused to stay the injunction — and refused to shrink it to the states that sued. On Sept. 10 the court denied DOJ’s emergency motions in full: “appellants have not made a strong showing that the district court erred in determining that the Final Rule is likely unlawful,” and they had not shown “that it is likely that Congress would delegate constitutionally assigned tasks relevant to maintaining our electoral system to the Postal Service through the ‘wafer-thin reed’ of a general grant of authority to manage the postal system.” The court also found “no record evidence of past fraud or a likelihood of impending fraud related to the November 3 election.” Notably, it rejected DOJ’s alternative request to narrow the injunction to the plaintiff states under Trump v. CASA — which would have fractured mail-ballot standards across the country and made every state’s protection contingent on whether its attorney general had joined a lawsuit. Arizona’s coverage never had to depend on its plaintiff status.
  • A Trump-appointed judge went further than the states’ own injunction. On Sunday, Sept. 13, Judge Carl Nichols (D.D.C.) issued a second preliminary injunction, in the case brought by the Democratic campaign committees together with LULAC and the NAACP. He rested entirely on statutory authority rather than the Constitution — the rule exceeds “any conception of the outer bounds” of Postal Service power — and barred implementation outright, including voluntary state compliance, reasoning that a patchwork would “create even more confusion and misunderstanding (and thus delays and mistakes).” He wrote that “the harm of untransmitted ballots — both to voters and candidates — is irreversible, because ‘once the election occurs, there can be no do-over and no redress,’” and rejected DOJ’s analogy between ballot mail and the Postal Service’s rules for cremated remains and replica explosives. Two independent injunctions, from judges appointed by different presidents, on different legal theories, is a sturdier shield than one (Injunction, D.D.C. ECF 192 (primary PDF) · NBC News (Sept. 14)).
  • Republican secretaries of state told the Supreme Court not to let the rule take effect — including from two states that were defending it. The amicus brief filed Sept. 9 by state and local election officials carried more than three dozen signatures across party lines, among them Georgia’s Brad Raffensperger, Kentucky’s Michael Adams, North Dakota’s Michael Howe, Utah Lt. Gov. Deidre Henderson, South Dakota’s Monae Johnson and Kansas’s Scott Schwab — the last two signing even though their states were among the twelve Republican-led states intervening in support of the rule. Their argument was administrative, not partisan: “In many respects, the election is already underway. Ballots have been prepared and proofed,” and “a mail ballot that is never delivered disadvantages no party in particular; it simply denies the franchise to an eligible voter who has followed state law in every respect.” Among the signers was former Maricopa County Recorder Helen Purcell — the Purcell of Purcell v. Gonzalez, the case whose name is invoked whenever courts are told not to change election rules close to an election. The American Postal Workers Union filed separately, telling the Court the employees who would run envelope review and voter-data verification had not been trained.
  • The Supreme Court declined three separate invitations to override a state supreme court on congressional maps. Missouri asked the justices to let it use a map its own high court had unanimously held was suspended by a citizen referendum petition. Justice Kavanaugh denied the stay outright on Sept. 8 (No. 26A304), without referring it to the full Court. When a federal district judge then ordered the state to use the map anyway and the Eighth Circuit declined to intervene, the full Court granted a stay of that order on Sept. 10 in People Not Politicians v. Onder, No. 26A326 — with no noted dissents — and Missouri’s attorney general confirmed the 2022 map will be used on Nov. 3. For Arizona, whose districts come from a commission created by citizen initiative and defended in state court, the result is reassuring on both halves of the mechanism: a state high court reading its own constitution was left undisturbed, and a federal court that tried to displace it was stopped (SCOTUSblog (Sept. 10) · St. Louis Public Radio (Sept. 10)).
  • The institutions that would have to absorb a ballot seizure are preparing before it happens, not after. UCLA’s Safeguarding Democracy Project published a guide on Sept. 9 for judges evaluating election-related search-warrant affidavits, urging them to bring election administrators into the conversation, to hold the matter on the record rather than in the usual ex parte secrecy, and to consider alternatives — waiting until after certification, accepting copies, or allowing observation of a recount — before ever breaking a chain of custody. The Brennan Center published companion guidance on guardrails against rubber-stamping such warrants; retired judges including former Ohio Chief Justice Maureen O’Connor and former District of Nevada Chief Judge Philip Pro are working through Keep Our Republic to reach the bench; and at least one state election office has run three statewide trainings this year. Separately, the Postal Service inspector general confirmed on Sept. 8 that it is conducting an independent review of the ballot-screening whistleblower’s allegations, eight days after they became public. The pattern worth noting is speed: courts, inspectors general and civil-society groups are now reacting in days rather than months (NPR (Sept. 9) · Brennan Center).
  • The nine-state federal voter hunt has produced about thirty subpoenaed names. Set against an administration framing built on 24,000-plus and even 278,000 claimed noncitizen registrations, the investigative demands actually reported are: 13 individuals in Georgia (Sept. 1), 2 in Washington, and roughly 15 in Wisconsin (Sept. 3). That is the measurable yield of hundreds of reassigned agents across three of the nine states, and it is the single most useful pre-buttal figure available to Arizona business leaders asked whether noncitizen voting is a real threat to the November count. (MS NOW (Sept. 4))
  • A state supreme court unanimously protected the citizen referendum right against its own legislature — the ruling now under attack at the Supreme Court. On Sept. 3 the Missouri Supreme Court held, without dissent, that “the plain language of … the Missouri Constitution authorizes a referendum as to ‘any act of the general assembly’ and no exception applies,” that the petition “was legal, sufficient, and timely,” and that the legislature’s new congressional map cannot take effect “unless and until approved by the voters.” It is a clean statement of the principle Arizona’s own independent redistricting commission rests on. The caveat is the reason it is also tracked as a risk: Missouri has asked the U.S. Supreme Court to stay it. (SCOTUSblog · Missouri Independent)
  • Arizona’s Secretary of State is running daily voter-information counter-programming through Election Day. On Sept. 4 the office launched “60 Days. 60 Facts.” at Arizona.Vote, publishing verified facts on the time, place and manner of voting every day until Nov. 3 — Fontes’s framing is that voters “should have straightforward information they can trust.” Pre-positioning accurate mechanics before a contested count is exactly the discipline that was missing in 2020 and 2022, and it gives LNP-AZ members a neutral, citable source to point employees and peers toward. (Arizona Secretary of State (Sept. 4))
  • A federal court blocked the USPS mail-ballot rule through Election Day — in a case Arizona brought. On Sept. 4 Judge Indira Talwani granted a preliminary injunction barring USPS from requiring states to comply with the envelope-approval and portal provisions for the 2026 midterms, and denied a stay pending appeal. Her findings are unusually direct: implementation “less than seventy days before the November 2026 election threatens disenfranchisement of millions”; “the record includes no evidence relating to fraudulent mail voting to support the rushed implementation”; and the rule is “[u]nauthorized by the Elections Clause… and is unconstitutional.” Arizona is a named plaintiff through AG Mayes, and the order covers the Oct. 7 early-ballot mailing. Caveat: it is not final — DOJ appealed the same day and an emergency application is pending at the Supreme Court. (Injunction (primary, PDF) · NBC News · ACLU)
  • Neither a state supreme court nor a U.S. Supreme Court Justice would order a ballot measure onto a ballot on an emergency basis. On Sept. 3 Justice Kavanaugh denied Americans for Citizen Voting–Michigan’s application (No. 26A275), and the Michigan Supreme Court denied relief the same day, holding the Board of State Canvassers had discretion and no ministerial duty to certify. The Board finalized Michigan’s ballot without the proof-of-citizenship measure on Sept. 4. The margin was three signatures — 626 valid against 629 required in the sample. The template matters more than the outcome: courts declining last-minute federal intervention in state ballot-access decisions is the same reflex that resolved both Arizona Prop. 144 challenges in August. (Democracy Docket · Votebeat Michigan)
  • Republican county recorders refused to act on unreliable state data. In the “HAVE YOU MOVED?” mailing, Yavapai Recorder Michelle Burchill (R) declined to send notices from a report her office judged unreliable — her preliminary review put roughly half the flagged address changes down as false positives — and said she will process the list only after the election, once she has a workable version. Gila County is also holding off. This is the same recorder whose full-roll SAVE audit in August found zero noncitizens among 255 federal-only voters. A Republican election official declining to generate voter confusion from bad data is a small thing that prevents a large one. (Votebeat Arizona · Arizona Mirror)
  • Congress engaged the USPS whistleblower disclosure within a day, and a watchdog appears to have opened a review. House Oversight ranking member Robert Garcia and Rep. Joe Morelle wrote the USPS Inspector General on Sept. 2 demanding an immediate probe; Garcia said on Sept. 4 the IG had informed them an investigation was opening. Notably, neither DOJ nor USPS has denied the substance of the disclosure in any court filing — as CEIR’s David Becker put it, “We’ve seen no denial from the Postal Service that the whistleblower said anything that was inaccurate.” The IG opening is single-source — a member’s characterization, not confirmed by the IG’s office. (Garcia–Morelle letter (primary, PDF) · Democracy Docket)
  • A targeted organization answered a federal referral with a court record, same day. Hours after DHS Secretary Mullin’s letter asking DOJ to investigate ERIC and CEIR became public on Sept. 4, CEIR executive director David Becker rebutted it point by point — CEIR does not receive motor-vehicle or voter data on an ongoing basis, does not condition grants on access to it, and was never contacted by DHS before the letter issued — and pointed to a June 2026 ruling by Judge William Conley (W.D. Wis.) dismissing a materially similar Driver’s Privacy Protection Act suit against ERIC and Becker and granting sanctions against plaintiffs’ counsel. Answering an allegation with an adverse judgment already on the books is a usable template for any Arizona official who receives a similar letter. (CEIR statement (primary) · Democracy Docket)
  • The Chairman of the Joint Chiefs rules out troops at polling places — in writing. On Aug. 31, Gen. Dan Caine responded in writing to Sen. Elissa Slotkin: “The Joint Force has no plans to send Federal military personnel or Federalized members of the National Guard to polling places during the 2026 elections,” no plans to seize ballots, machines or election material, and he has “neither received nor anticipate receiving any unlawful order” regarding the November midterms. That directly answers one of the roughly 100 scenarios on the Senate task force threat list this dashboard logged on Aug. 31. The caveat worth keeping: Secretary Hegseth has not responded to the parallel request, and DHS’s separate assurance is only that “ICE is not planning operations targeting polling locations.” (Slotkin release with Caine letter (Aug. 31) · PBS NewsHour · The Hill · NBC News)
  • An independent state auditor clears a battleground voter roll at 99.99% — the best available pre-buttal template for Arizona. Michigan’s nonpartisan Office of the Auditor General reported (released Aug. 28, widely covered Sept. 2) that 99.99% of the 8.3 million voters on the state’s Qualified Voter File had complete and valid data, and that routine list maintenance is in fact happening — nearly one million lawful cancellations between January 2022 and March 2026. It also resolves the two figures that travel fastest: of 2,582 ballots associated with decedents, 2,561 were early or absentee ballots cast by people who were alive when they voted and died before Election Day, against a Michigan baseline of about 281 deaths a day; and citizenship-related cancellations totaled 403. Expect the raw counts (403, 653 registrants with implausible birthdates, 2,582) to be quoted in Arizona stripped of that context — the audit itself is the answer. (Votebeat Michigan (Sept. 2) · Michigan Auditor General report (primary, PDF))
  • Fewer than half of Republican attorneys general would defend the USPS mail-ballot rule in court. Reported Aug. 31: when the Trump administration sought state support for the rule in the District of Massachusetts, fewer than half of Republican state AGs signed on — twelve intervened. In a fight the administration has framed as red states against blue states, the majority of Republican AGs declined to put their names on a federal rule that would reach into how their own states mail ballots. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 31))
  • The Senate created its first-ever election observer program, and the scenario planning is now institutional rather than ad hoc. NPR reported on Aug. 28 that Sen. Alex Padilla has launched the first Senate Election Observer Program, and that the Schumer-convened election task force — 11+ senators and roughly 30 staff meeting weekly — maintains a working list of about 100 threat scenarios with response plans attached. Election lawyer Marc Elias told NPR his firm is currently running 87 lawsuits across 43 states. Sen. Minority Leader McConnell’s office, which chairs Rules, declined to say whether Republican staff will participate. Whatever one makes of the partisan split, the pre-positioning of legal and observer capacity before Nov. 3 — rather than after a disputed count — is the difference between contesting a result and defending a process. (NPR (Aug. 28))
  • Arizona already does the post-election hand audit that DHS is now demanding as a grant condition. A.R.S. § 16-602 has required, after every primary, special, general and presidential election, a hand-count audit of at least 2% of a county’s precincts (or two precincts, whichever is greater) plus 1% of early ballots or 5,000, whichever is less — with counties free to audit more. So when DHS Secretary Mullin’s Aug. 20 letter conditioned full Homeland Security Grant Program awards on “random post-election manual counts of paper ballots,” Arizona was already compliant by statute, not by scramble. Long-standing state audit law is what turns a federal ultimatum into paperwork. (A.R.S. § 16-602 · Verified Voting · AZ SOS hand-count audit summaries)
  • A federal judge froze the USPS mail-ballot rule for the entire November election — the day after USPS said it had taken effect. On Aug. 27, U.S. District Judge Indira Talwani (D. Mass.) issued a 14-day temporary restraining order blocking the core provisions of the Postal Service’s final “Ballot Mail for Federal Elections” rule — envelope-design mandates, the USPS portal registration requirement, the state voter-list upload, and the refusal-to-deliver authority — for any election held on or before Nov. 3, 2026, and barred USPS from taking “further preparatory steps.” She found no congressional delegation permitting USPS to regulate election mail and wrote that the record “continues to lack any evidence regarding fraudulent absentee or mail-in voting.” The order covers both the private plaintiffs’ case and the states’ new suit. It is temporary — a hearing on longer-term relief is set for Sept. 3 — but for now Arizona’s ~80% mail electorate is protected through the general. (CNN · NBC News)
  • Arizona is a named plaintiff defending its own mail voting. On Aug. 26, AG Kris Mayes joined 24 attorneys general and Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro in suing the U.S. Postal Service in the District of Massachusetts over the final ballot-mail rule, filing with an emergency TRO motion the same day the rule was published in the Federal Register. “The Constitution is crystal clear: states run their elections — not the postal service and not the President.” The coalition’s motion contributed to the Aug. 27 order above. This is Arizona’s second multistate election suit of the cycle. (AG Mayes release · KJZZ)
  • A second state’s Republican chief election officer got the federal government to admit its noncitizen numbers don’t hold up. Pennsylvania Secretary of the Commonwealth Al Schmidt (R) disclosed in an Aug. 25 letter that DHS conceded at an Aug. 13 meeting it lacked “high confidence” in its claim that 14,576 Pennsylvanians were noncitizen registrants — following Nevada, where a ~16,000 claim shrank to 185 records DHS stood behind. Two states that checked, two collapses. It is a template Arizona has not yet used. (Democracy Docket)
  • The second injunction held — and the judge who issued it found the administration broke it. The Supreme Court’s Aug. 24 stay reached only the 23-state California case; it left untouched Judge Indira Talwani’s Aug. 11 nationwide preliminary injunction in League of Women Voters of Massachusetts (D. Mass.), which still bars USPS from implementing the ballot-mail rule in every state, Arizona included. When USPS finalized the rule anyway on Aug. 21, the League moved to enforce, and on Aug. 25 Talwani wrote that “[d]efendants cannot contend that they misunderstood the scope of the court’s order” and found they “violated the preliminary injunction in this case.” Caveat, and it is a real one: she declined every requested remedy, entered no contempt or sanctions, allowed publication to proceed, and did not amend the injunction to expressly reach the now-final rule — so this is a shield that held, not a shield that was reinforced, and it is a single district-court order under active appeal. USPS has separately said it will not implement for November absent relief from both injunctions. (Axios (Aug. 25) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 22) · SCOTUSblog (Aug. 24))
  • DHS privately conceded its noncitizen voter-roll numbers were inflated. NPR reported Aug. 25 that after publicly claiming roughly 16,000 noncitizens on Nevada’s voter rolls, a DHS official called that figure “the ceiling” in an Aug. 13 meeting with state officials and the next day produced a list of only 185 records the department was confident about. A Pennsylvania official said “it was clear that DHS did not have confidence that the numbers they have stated publicly were accurate.” The admission undercuts Secretary Mullin’s July assertion of ~250,000 noncitizens registered across four states and supports what Arizona officials have said about SAVE-derived flags all along. (Single-outlet: NPR; DHS declined to comment.) (NPR (Aug. 25) · WFAE (Aug. 25))
  • A Republican Arizona recorder ran her whole roll through SAVE — and the result was a rounding error, plus 255 voters upgraded to a full ballot. Yavapai County Recorder Michelle Burchill (R) checked 193,114 registrations (99.8% of her roll) against the federal SAVE system and published her findings Aug. 7 (reported by Votebeat Aug. 22): zero noncitizens among the 255 federal-only voters she reviewed — all 255 were confirmed citizens and converted to full-ballot status, an access gain — and, after independent vetting, 30 cancellations, about 0.016% of county voters. A Republican official’s own audit putting the rate two orders of magnitude below federal claims is the most credible Arizona-specific rebuttal available, and her two process flags (MVD citizenship changes reaching no one, and no guidance for the NVRA quiet period) are fixable before November. (Votebeat Arizona (Aug. 22) · Burchill report (PDF))
  • Fontes beat back the Miller-founded America First Legal at every level — the Prop. 144 ballot-language fight is over. After Judge Adele Ponce dismissed AFL’s suit Aug. 13 (Fontes’s description is “neither false nor clearly misleading”; the three voter-plaintiffs lacked standing), the Arizona Supreme Court rejected AFL’s appeal on Aug. 19 (No. CV-26-0272): Chief Justice Timmer, joined by Justices Lopez, Beene, and Cruz, held the plaintiffs’ objections were “shared with many voters across the state” and “certainly do not constitute a distinct and palpable injury,” and that the wording sits within the Secretary’s statutory discretion (Montgomery and King dissented in part; the court was unanimous in upholding the “may make it harder to challenge election laws” line). The Aug. 20 ballot-finalization deadline then passed with Fontes’s language intact — both Prop. 144 legal challenges (Keating’s single-subject attack and AFL’s wording attack) are now fully resolved in the state’s favor, and the national election-litigation apparatus leaves Arizona’s ballot administration empty-handed. This item has been retired from the active tracker. (Arizona Mirror (Aug. 19) · Arizona Capitol Times (Aug. 19) · KJZZ (Aug. 19) · Tucson Sentinel (Aug. 19))
  • The Navajo County “undeliverable ballots” scare deflated under scrutiny. A Aug. 19 Votebeat data investigation found undelivered mail ballots are routine and statewide — at least 50,000 in the July primary across 11 reporting counties, consistent with past cycles — and Navajo County’s 3.9% rate is within historical norms; the AG’s office said “we don’t believe there’s anything nefarious going on there,” and a USPS sample review traced the returns to change-of-address orders, vacant addresses, and closed PO boxes. Navajo County committed to forwardable notices and direct voter outreach before November (see the Navajo County item, stepped down to Emerging). (Votebeat (Aug. 19) · Arizona Mirror (Aug. 19))
  • A federal judge blocked Trump’s mail-ballot order nationwide — a second, independent shield. On Aug. 11, U.S. District Judge Indira Talwani granted voting-rights groups’ renewed preliminary injunction barring USPS and the federal government from implementing EO 14399’s mail-voting provisions for the Nov. 3 midterms in every state — not just the 23 that sued — finding the order likely unconstitutional and noting the government “declined to defend” it. Unlike the July 2021 NAACP order (stayed on appeal July 17), this injunction is fresh and independent of the pending Supreme Court stay applications, so even a SCOTUS stay of the earlier California injunction would not automatically revive the EO. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 11) · NPR (Aug. 11) · Axios (Aug. 11))
  • Arizona's July 21 primary ran cleanly — the feared meltdown didn't happen. Maricopa County reported no equipment failures and no lines across all 236 vote centers (only minor, quickly-resolved hiccups); the settled Recorder–Board split and the Arizona Supreme Court's 12-point interim plan ran without a mid-election dispute; the 2024 faster-count option (used by ~30% of Maricopa voters) helped AP call the marquee statewide primaries within ~20 minutes of the 8 p.m. results drop; DOJ's monitors in Maricopa, Pima, and Apache produced no incidents or charges and ICE did not appear at polling places; and no losing candidate alleged fraud (Gina Swoboda conceded graciously). It is the strongest single-day evidence this cycle that Arizona's election machinery works under pressure. (KTAR · Votebeat · AP via Yahoo)
  • All 15 Arizona counties certified the July 21 primary — cleanly, and on time. Every county board completed its canvass by the Aug. 3 deadline with no delay, refusal, or protest — a sharp contrast with the 2022 Cochise County standoff. Maricopa certified unanimously Aug. 3, with Supervisor Thomas Galvin stressing “no allegations of fraud, no allegations of vote rigging, no allegations of fixed elections,” and Yavapai’s board approved its canvass unanimously the same day; no automatic recount was triggered. Secretary of State Fontes then certified the statewide canvass on Aug. 6, formally completing primary certification with all 15 counties in — Apache and La Paz also completed successful hand-count audits, and Apache moved to a vote-center model. (KJZZ (Aug. 3) · KJZZ (Aug. 6, statewide canvass) · Prescott Daily Courier (Aug. 3))
  • Arizona joins a 25-state coalition suing to block Trump's election conditions on homeland-security grants. On July 23, AG Kris Mayes and 24 other states plus D.C. (led by Rhode Island, with California, New Jersey, and Illinois) sued FEMA and DHS in federal court to stop the administration from conditioning the $1.06 billion Homeland Security Grant Program on SAVE citizenship checks of the entire voter roll, a forced transition to hand-marked paper ballots, and federally prescribed audits — arguing the conditions violate the Administrative Procedure Act and the Spending Clause because Congress never authorized DHS or FEMA to rewrite state election law. The filing lands one day before the July 24 application deadline; the coalition estimates about $148 million (a 20% holdback) is at stake across the plaintiff states. Update (Aug. 18): a second front opened when four localities — Nashville/Davidson County, Harris County (TX), El Paso County (TX), and Columbus (OH) — filed their own suit against the same DHS/FEMA scheme (counsel including the Brennan Center and Protect Democracy), adding to the pressure to block the conditions before they bite this fall. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 18) · CBS News (July 23) · Reuters/U.S. News (July 23) · Courthouse News (July 23))
  • Arizona officials move to ban election betting by government insiders. On July 16, SOS Fontes barred his staff from wagering on elections or using nonpublic, job-acquired information on prediction markets like Kalshi and Polymarket — violations mean termination and law-enforcement referral — and the Maricopa Board of Supervisors unanimously adopted a prediction-market ban covering the county’s ~13,000 employees, following Gov. Hobbs’s similar executive order the prior week. (KTAR · AZ Capitol Times · AZFamily)
  • The bipartisan faster-count law passed its first test July 21. Under the 2024 law, voters who drop off early ballots with photo ID and an envelope barcode skip signature verification — which should speed Arizona’s famously slow results — and Maricopa County livestreamed its logic-and-accuracy testing with observers from all recognized parties. Roughly 755,000 early ballots had already been cast statewide as of July 16 (~335K D / ~347K R), per AP (AP Decision Notes). And the option proved popular in practice: Votebeat reported July 30 that about 23,000 Maricopa voters — roughly 30% of Election Day drop-offs — and 6,000+ in Pima used the ID-verify option, letting Recorder Heap’s office finish signature verification about 13 hours after polls closed, a potential preview of faster November results (Votebeat (July 30)). (12News · Fox 10)
  • Federal judge quashes DOJ subpoena targeting 2020 election workers. On July 7, U.S. District Judge William Ray II (N.D. Ga., a Trump appointee) quashed the DOJ grand jury subpoena demanding the names, addresses, and cell phone numbers of Fulton County 2020 election workers — calling the demand “staggering,” warning it “threatens to chill participation in future elections,” and noting the statute of limitations on any 2020 offense has expired. It is the first judicial rejection of the 2020 re-investigation’s subpoenas, and a directly citable precedent for the parallel Arizona track. (CNN · The Hill · Bloomberg)
  • Federal judge blocks USCIS from barring voter-registration groups at naturalization ceremonies. On Aug. 3 (reported Aug. 4), U.S. District Judge Adam Abelson temporarily blocked a Department of Homeland Security (USCIS) rule that had barred the League of Women Voters and other nonpartisan groups from registering newly naturalized citizens to vote at USCIS ceremonies — finding a likely First Amendment violation and likely discriminatory intent. A national ruling that rolls back a federal-agency restriction on voter registration and applies in Arizona (single-outlet coverage as of this compile). (Votebeat (Aug. 4))
  • Second court blocks the USPS mail-ballot scheme — nationwide (stayed on appeal July 17). On July 1, U.S. District Judge Emmet Sullivan (D.D.C.) granted the NAACP's motion to enforce its December 2021 settlement with USPS — which requires prioritized, timely ballot delivery through at least 2028 — ruling the proposed "no list, no delivery" scheme would violate it. Caveat: a D.C. Circuit panel stayed this order July 17 pending appeal (see the mail-ballot EO item), so the Boston injunction covering Arizona is the shield currently in effect. (Reuters/U.S. News · The Hill · Democracy Docket)
  • Arizona Supreme Court upholds the Voters' Right to Know Act (Prop. 211) dark-money disclosure law. On June 29, the Court ruled 4–3 (Chief Justice Timmer writing for the majority) that the 2022 voter-approved law requiring disclosure of major donors behind campaign spending does not violate donors' privacy rights, rejecting the primary challenge — though it remanded a narrower free-speech/chilling-effect claim to the trial court for further proceedings. A win for campaign-finance transparency heading into the primary, with one claim still open. (KTAR · Arizona Mirror · KJZZ)
  • Supreme Court upholds late-arriving mail ballots, rejecting the Trump/RNC challenge. On June 29, in Watson v. RNC (No. 24-1260), the Court ruled 5–4 (Justice Barrett writing, joined by Chief Justice Roberts and the three liberal justices) that states may count mail ballots postmarked by Election Day but received afterward, upholding Mississippi's five-day grace period and similar laws in 14 states and D.C. Arizona is not a postmark-grace-period state (it requires ballots to be received by 7 p.m. on Election Day), so there is no direct operational change here — but the ruling forecloses a major national attack line on mail-ballot counting heading into the midterms. (CBS News · Democracy Docket · Opinion (June 29))
  • Boston court's block on Trump's mail-voting order confirmed to cover Arizona. Judge Indira Talwani's June 25 injunction against core provisions of EO 14399 (State of California v. Trump) explicitly covers Arizona — one of 24 jurisdictions that sued — blocking the June 30 federal citizenship-list rollout and the USPS "no list, no delivery" rule as applied to Arizona. The relief is limited to the 2026 cycle (challenges to future elections were dismissed as unripe) — and the administration has now appealed to the First Circuit and asked the district court to lift the injunction by July 6 — and on July 7 Judge Talwani refused, ruling that having to “work harder later” is not irreparable injury — and on July 25 the First Circuit denied the administration’s motion to stay that injunction pending appeal (2–1), keeping the Boston order covering Arizona fully in force for the 2026 general (see the mail-ballot EO item). (CBS News (July 25) · Votebeat · NPR · Bloomberg Law)
  • Sixth Circuit hands DOJ its first appellate loss on voter-roll demands. On June 24, the Sixth Circuit affirmed dismissal of the Justice Department's suit to compel Michigan's full voter roll — the first federal appeals-court ruling against DOJ's nationwide voter-data campaign, a precedent Arizona's own pending appeal (after the April dismissal of the DOJ suit here) can cite. A Pennsylvania federal judge added to the trend on June 27, dismissing DOJ's suit for that state's voter roll as a "fishing expedition" — by Votebeat's count federal judges have now ruled against the DOJ voter-data campaign in 10 states. And on July 6 the First Circuit refused to fast-track or consolidate DOJ’s Maine/Massachusetts/Rhode Island voter-roll appeals — denying DOJ’s bid to finish briefing before the NVRA quiet period begins; the campaign stood at 0–11 in district courts and 0–1 on appeal — and then fell further: on July 10 a N.D.N.Y. judge dismissed DOJ’s suit for New York’s unredacted rolls (0–12 in district courts by Democracy Docket’s count), DOJ petitioned the full Sixth Circuit to rehear its Michigan loss en banc, and new research reported July 9 found state voter rolls largely accurate and up to date. Three more dismissals followed — West Virginia (July 13), then Virginia and New Mexico (July 14, both before GOP-appointed judges) — extending DOJ’s losing streak to 0–15 — and on July 17, U.S. District Judge Kari Dooley, a Trump appointee, dismissed the Connecticut suit, ruling Title III of the Civil Rights Act of 1960 doesn’t reach state voter-registration lists: the campaign stood at 0–16 in district courts (0–1 on appeal), with seven Trump appointees among the judges ruling against DOJ. And on July 23, U.S. District Judge Claria Horn Boom (a Trump appointee, E.D. Ky.) dismissed the Kentucky suit with prejudice and denied DOJ's motion to compel, expressly citing the Sixth Circuit's Michigan ruling — extending the streak to 0–17 in district courts (still 0–1 on appeal) (Democracy Docket (July 17, Connecticut) · Kentucky Lantern (July 23, Kentucky) · Louisville Public Media (July 24)). The streak then reached 0–18 when a New Jersey suit was dismissed with prejudice July 29 (Judge Zahid Quraishi) and 0–19 when Illinois’s was tossed July 31 (Judge Colleen Lawless), then 0–20 when the Colorado suit was dismissed with prejudice Aug. 4 (Judge Philip Brimmer, a George W. Bush appointee who expressly cited the Sixth Circuit), and 0–21 when U.S. District Judge Randolph Moss (D.D.C.) dismissed the DOJ’s demand for the District of Columbia’s full voter list on Aug. 6 — holding the 1960 Civil Rights Act lets DOJ request individual registrations but not a jurisdiction’s entire live roll — then 0–22 when U.S. District Judge Anne Traum (D. Nev.) dismissed the Nevada suit and denied DOJ’s motion to compel on Aug. 14 — the same day the full Sixth Circuit declined to rehear its Michigan loss en banc (United States v. Benson), leaving DOJ’s first appellate defeat intact. The streak then reached 0–23 when U.S. District Judge Katherine Menendez (D. Minn.) dismissed the DOJ’s suit for Minnesota’s voter data on Aug. 17, calling it an impermissible “fishing expedition” — with a Washington State dismissal (heard Aug. 4 before Judge Kymberly Evanson, ruling still pending) potentially set to make it 0–24 — and on Sept. 1 New Hampshire Secretary of State David Scanlan (R) asked the First Circuit to dispose of DOJ’s appeal without full briefing, arguing “there is no substantial question as to the facial insufficiency of the federal government’s demand”; Voting Rights Lab’s Sept. 2 tally puts the district-court losses at over 20 and confirms the Sixth Circuit has already denied DOJ’s appeal (Democracy Docket (Sept. 1, New Hampshire) · UW State Democracy Research Initiative tracker). Even so, the DOJ signaled it will not stand down: AG Todd Blanche (NBC, Aug. 16) called a Supreme Court appeal “a possibility” and said “we’re not going to stop,” and AAG Harmeet Dhillon (Aug. 17) echoed it — so the losing streak is a bright spot now shadowed by a threatened escalation (Democracy Docket (Aug. 6, D.C.) · Washington Times (Aug. 6) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 14, Nevada 0–22) · Las Vegas Review-Journal (Aug. 14) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 14, Sixth Circuit en banc) · Minnesota Reformer (Aug. 17) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 17, Minnesota 0–23) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 16, Blanche/SCOTUS)) — all tracking the Sixth Circuit’s Michigan reasoning (Democracy Docket (July 29, NJ) · Democracy Docket (July 31, IL) · Democracy Docket (Aug. 4, CO)). (Votebeat (Michigan) · The Hill · Votebeat (Pennsylvania, June 28) · Democracy Docket (July 6, First Circuit) · Democracy Docket (July 11, New York) · Democracy Docket (en banc) · Democracy Docket (July 9, rolls research) · Democracy Docket (July 13, West Virginia) · Democracy Docket (July 14, Virginia & New Mexico))
  • The federal 90-day “quiet period” barring systematic voter purges took effect Aug. 5. Under the National Voter Registration Act, states may not conduct systematic voter-list removals within 90 days of a federal election — a window that opened Aug. 5 ahead of Nov. 3 and legally constrains any late SAVE-driven purge. The same day, Sen. Alex Padilla led Senate Democrats in a letter pressing the DOJ to confirm NVRA compliance and drop its voter-roll suits. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 5) · Sen. Padilla (Aug. 5))
  • Court blocks the federal SAVE-database overhaul as unlawful. On June 22, a D.D.C. judge ruled the administration's 2025 expansion of the SAVE / immigration-status system — used to flag alleged noncitizen voters — violated the Privacy Act, Social Security Act, and APA. DHS vowed to appeal, so the win is provisional. (Votebeat · NPR)
  • Bipartisan FY2027 budget signed into law, averting a shutdown before the primary. Gov. Hobbs signed the ~$18.3B bipartisan budget on June 13, ending the standoff that had risked a July 1 government shutdown colliding with early voting. (Caveat: whether it cures the county HAVA appropriation lapsing June 30 is unconfirmed — see the Election Funding item.) (AZ Capitol Times · Arizona Mirror)
  • DHS retreats on mail-voter data harvesting. A June 8 DOJ filing disclosed DHS dropped both the SAVE database and a planned voter-roll upload portal from mail-ballot EO implementation, and walked back direct USPS data integration — though experts caution the retreat may be litigation strategy, and federal citizenship lists are still slated for June 30. (Arizona Mirror)
  • SAVE Act blocked in the Senate. On June 4, Senators Collins, Murkowski, McConnell, and Tillis joined all Democrats to defeat the documentary proof-of-citizenship mandate; Leader Thune signaled it won't return this session — and as of June 19 Thune is rebuffing Trump's new demand to weld SAVE to FISA §702 renewal, calling it "unrealistic" (see Legislative pillar above). (NPR · NPR (June 17)) Update (Aug. 8): the Senate left for its five-week August recess early Aug. 8 without passing the SAVE Act — a narrower voter-ID substitute (Sen. Husted’s bill) failed 52–46, and the full SAVE Act plus its reconciliation vehicle were deferred to September. (Democracy Docket (Aug. 8))
  • Bill introduced to bar federal interference at the polls. A bipartisan-targeted "Protect Our Polls Act" (led by Sen. Elissa Slotkin, with Arizona Sens. Mark Kelly and Ruben Gallego among co-sponsors) was introduced June 18 to require congressional approval before deploying uniformed military or federal law enforcement to polling places and to cut funding for any federal seizure of ballots or voting machines — a direct counter to the polling-site enforcement proposals tracked above. Passage prospects are uncertain in the current Congress, though the push continues — Sen. Jacky Rosen (D-NV) publicly joined the effort July 14 (Las Vegas Sun). A companion measure, the Voting Systems Protection Act — co-introduced June 18 by Rep. Dan Goldman and Arizona Rep. Yassamin Ansari — would guard against federal seizure of voting systems and election infrastructure (Rep. Goldman release). (Sen. Kelly release · KTAR (June 18))
  • DOJ voter-roll lawsuit dismissed with prejudice. On April 28, U.S. District Judge Susan Brnovich — a Trump appointee — dismissed DOJ's suit seeking Arizona's full unredacted voter file. (Votebeat)
  • Mass-purge suit tossed on appeal. The 9th Circuit affirmed dismissal of the suit claiming up to 1.27 million ineligible Arizona voters, finding "no proof that there are ineligible people on the rolls." (Arizona Mirror)
  • Bipartisan primary date change in effect. The primary moved from August 4 to July 21, 2026, giving officials time to meet military and overseas ballot deadlines even after a statewide recount. (Votebeat)
  • EPM creation process upheld — opinion now published. The Arizona Supreme Court and Court of Appeals fully rejected the GOP challenge to how the Elections Procedures Manual is made (Oct.–Dec. 2025), and on July 17, 2026 the Supreme Court released its unanimous opinion explaining the ruling: the EPM is exempt from the state APA, so no public comment period is required (see the rulebook item). (Arizona Mirror · Arizona Mirror (July 17))

Compiled September 16, 2026 by Leadership Now Arizona (current through Wednesday morning, September 16; covers the window since September 15). Key dates: statewide primary held July 21, 2026 (fully certified) · No. 26A308 — fully briefed at the Supreme Court since 4 p.m. Sept. 15; an order may issue any day · Sept. 19 — Arizona’s UOCAVA military and overseas ballot deadline · Sept. 21 — DHS response deadline on the USCIS whistleblower disclosure · Sept. 23 / Sept. 30 / Oct. 6 — Clean Elections Attorney General, Secretary of State and Governor debates · Sept. 24 – Oct. 29 — window for the Clean Elections repayment recommendation · registration deadline Oct. 5 · early voting begins and early ballots mail Oct. 7 · Oct. 13 — respondents’ merits briefs in RNC v. Mi Familia Vota, and comments close on the Census apportionment rule · Dec. 8 — oral argument in RNC v. Mi Familia Vota · Dec. 11 — continuing resolution expires, FY2027 HAVA decided · general election November 3, 2026 (Governor, Secretary of State, and Attorney General all on the ballot — 48 days out). (1) HEADLINE — the federal-officers-at-the-polls question is now an FBI question, and the Bureau’s answer changed inside one hearing. At Senate Judiciary oversight on Sept. 15, Director Kash Patel told Sen. Klobuchar he would follow the federal guidelines barring agents from open polling places, then repeatedly declined to rule out deploying them — “I don’t know that we can’t legally do it” — against 18 U.S.C. § 592. Two weeks after DHS Secretary Mullin said the same thing about ICE, two federal agencies have now declined to foreclose it, and the FBI runs election-crime coordinators out of all 56 field offices including Phoenix. Added Elevated; no deployment has been ordered and no written FBI polling-place policy exists in evidence. (2) The government partially confirmed the whistleblower disclosure in its own court filing. A DOJ notice filed Sept. 14 with Judge Sooknanan (D.D.C.) states that DHS “reactivated” its link to Social Security Administration databases and used SSA data to help determine whether “illegal voting may have occurred” — filed while the administration’s application to restore SAVE, No. 26A308, sat fully briefed and undecided at the Supreme Court. Whether that pathway falls inside or outside her June 22 order is contested; status unconfirmed as to any violation. (3) The enforcement push produced its first charges — and its clearest error rate, on the same day. Seven people were charged in Tarrant County, Texas on Sept. 15 by the Texas AG, the U.S. Attorney for N.D. Tex., the FBI and HSI; hours later the Texas Secretary of State confirmed 578 voters its SAVE-generated list had flagged are citizens — 506 of them only because passport data had not been loaded into SAVE — and ordered them reinstated. Maricopa is the structural analog to Tarrant; the 578 figure is the most citable public evidence yet of how wrong these lists run. (4) Arizona itself was quiet. No new Arizona filing, ruling, hearing date, legislative action or county development surfaced in this window; the Maricopa recorder–board mediation, the Clean Elections clawback litigation and Prop. 144 are all unchanged since the Sept. 15 edition. Arizona remains off every federal enforcement list and has received no demand letter. (5) Bright spot: Arizona CEOs made the economic case for election legitimacy in the Arizona Capitol Times on Sept. 14, with 60 signatories on the Leadership Now statement. Tracker now at 53 items — 7 Critical, 27 Elevated, 19 Emerging. Next update: Friday, September 18, 2026.

Sources reviewed: 120+ published reports across primary government records, court filings, AZ news outlets, election-law publications, and democracy trackers. Next update: September 18, 2026.

This dashboard is produced by a cross-partisan group of election experts and former officials. Items are flagged Critical, Elevated, or Emerging based on imminence and impact. Every named person, date, claim, and statistic is sourced. Items whose current procedural status could not be confirmed as of compilation are noted in the text ("status unconfirmed") — verify before quoting in public-facing material.
